Once schedule_tasks had zero rows for Aug 29/30, those dates simply vanished from `scheduledDatesDesc` — and since the streak is a *count* of matched dates, not "days since a gap," removing 2 previously-completed dates from consideration reduced the count by exactly 2, for every student who'd done them, with no missed day on their part at all. Confirmed empirically: every 47-day-streak student showed exactly 45 immediately after the blank-Aug29/30 load. **The first fix tried — restoring the old "Revision" task text for just those two dates — was explicitly rejected on follow-up**: the schedule content itself (what actually shows on the checklist) must always match the source PDF exactly, never carry stale content just to protect a number computed from it. **The real fix: `streakScheduledDatesFor()` (new, `lib/supabase.js`), used by both `complete-task.js` and `daily-streak-snapshot.js` (the only two call sites that ever call `computeStreak()` — `streak.js`/`pomodoro-leaderboard.js`/`team-students.js` all just read the cached `students.current_streak` column now, see the streak-caching entry below) — unions each student's OWN completed `task_progress` dates into their OWN `scheduledDatesDesc` walk, per student, not a change to the global schedule.** This is always safe: a `task_progress` row can only ever exist for a task that was genuinely shown to that student on the checklist at some point (no other way to create one), so "I completed something on this date" is itself sufficient proof that date was a real checkpoint for *them* — independent of whatever `schedule_tasks` says about it now, and with zero effect on any student who never touched that date. No schema change needed, no manual SQL step, `schedule_tasks` stays byte-for-byte aligned with the real source schedule. Verified by force-running `daily-streak-snapshot.js`'s handler directly after reloading the blanked schedule and confirming all 8 originally-sampled students (47/47/47/47/47/47/46/24) matched their exact pre-change values again — including two unrelated students who *do* show a genuine streak of 0 post-fix, confirmed by walking their real completion history and finding an actual unrelated gap (missed Sep 12-15, days before any of this happened) rather than mistaking that for a regression. **General rule for next time**: never let schedule content diverge from the real source to protect a derived number — if a schedule reload would remove credit for an already-completed date, fix the *computation* (like this), not the *content*.
- **Local Supabase (`supabase start`) doesn't auto-grant table privileges** the way a hosted project does — a table created via migration has no `service_role` GRANTs by default on the local CLI's Postgres image, so every query fails with "permission denied" even with correct credentials. `supabase/schema.sql` ends with explicit `GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ... TO service_role` — required for local dev to work at all, keep it even though hosted Supabase might not strictly need it.
- **Any script run with `.env` loaded hits the real production Supabase project, not a sandbox** — there's no separate local/staging database (see "Local dev needs `netlify dev`, not Docker" above). Seeding synthetic test rows directly via `getSupabase()` (rather than through `netlify dev`'s served app) is easy to reach for when testing backend logic in isolation, but it's real, live data the instant it's written — confirmed the hard way: seeding 20 fake high-minute `pomodoro_stats` rows to test the leaderboard's below-top-20 behavior briefly *displaced actual students* from the real top-20 leaderboard until cleaned up. Prefer mocking the HTTP response (e.g. Playwright route interception) over seeding real rows whenever the goal is testing frontend rendering; if a backend query genuinely needs live-data verification, clean up every row written immediately after and verify the real leaderboard/data is back to its prior state, not just that the delete call returned success.
- **`.fade-in` elements + a `fullPage` Playwright screenshot taken without scrolling can look like missing content** when they're actually present and correct — the IntersectionObserver-driven reveal hasn't fired yet for anything below the fold at capture time. Before treating a screenshot gap as a real bug, either scroll through first or run `document.querySelectorAll('.fade-in').forEach(el => el.classList.add('visible'))` to force-reveal everything, then re-screenshot.
- **Regenerating a `.fade-in` element's `innerHTML` replays its entrance animation from scratch**, even if the only actual change was a number. Hit this repeatedly in `progress.js` (streak card, subject-breakdown, heatmap all did this on every task toggle). Fix pattern used throughout: patch the specific value in place (`el.textContent = ...`, `el.style.width = ...`, `el.setAttribute('data-level', ...)`) instead of regenerating and reassigning a `.fade-in` ancestor's `innerHTML`.
- **Don't try to recolor native `<input type="number">` spin buttons for a dark theme** — tried `color-scheme: dark`, then an explicit `::-webkit-inner-spin-button { filter: invert(1) }`, and the arrows were still low-contrast/black in the user's actual browser both times (couldn't even verify visually in headless Chromium, which renders/suppresses them differently than a real browser). WebKit/Firefox/Safari each handle these hooks inconsistently. Just remove them (`-webkit-appearance: none` on the spin-button pseudo-elements, `appearance`/`-moz-appearance: textfield` on the input) — typing a number directly or the keyboard up/down arrow keys still work with the visual spinner hidden.
- **`CLIENT_VERSION` (the matching constant in `progress.js` and `netlify/functions/lib/supabase.js`) needs bumping on every real client-behavior change to `progress.js`, not only when a request/response payload shape changes.** It drives `checkClientVersion()` in `progress.js`, which polls `/api/version` every 2 minutes and force-`location.reload()`s a tab on a mismatch — the whole point being that an already-open tab picks up a fix automatically instead of running stale JS indefinitely. It went unbumped for 33 days across several real behavior-changing commits (including a genuine race-condition fix to the Pomodoro timer), which silently disabled that safety net for the entire window: `/api/version` kept returning the same string, so the check never tripped, even though the code underneath had materially changed. Any tab left open during that gap never got reloaded onto the fixes. Bump both copies together whenever `progress.js`'s runtime logic changes in any way a student could notice, not just on contract changes.
- **`checkClientVersion()`'s and `scheduleMidnightReload()`'s force-`location.reload()` used to fire unconditionally, including on a backgrounded tab — which genuinely broke real, actively-running Pomodoro sessions, caught directly from a leaderboard anomaly on 2026-09-16 ("why is nobody in the top 10 today not live and suspiciously everyone ended their session 1,2,3 minutes ago").** A `CLIENT_VERSION` bump forces every open tab to reload on its next 2-minute poll — for a tab in the foreground this is a harmless, near-instant blip (the existing `wasHardReload`/`restorePomoActiveState` restore already handles it correctly). But `pagehide` (and therefore `sendPomoStoppedBeacon`'s "session stopped" signal, deliberately widened elsewhere in this file to fire on *every* teardown) fires immediately even for a backgrounded tab, while the reload's actual page-load-and-restore doesn't finish executing until the tab is foregrounded again, since browsers suspend/throttle JS in background tabs. A student who never touched anything — very plausibly the *majority* of students, per direct confirmation ("majority of people keep the pomodoro in the background while studying in other tabs") — ends up with their real, in-progress session parked at `running:false` server-side for as long as the tab stays backgrounded, looking exactly like they stopped when they didn't. Confirmed directly against real production data immediately after a `CLIENT_VERSION` bump: 8 of that day's top-10 students' `pomo_active_session` rows were all stuck at `running:false`, `updated_at` clustered within the same ~2-minute window right after the deploy, several genuinely mid-session (partial `seconds_left`, `phase_end_at` still in the future) — and still stuck 5+ minutes later with zero further sync activity, ruling out a self-healing blip. **Not something introduced by that day's other work** — `checkClientVersion()` and the beacon-on-every-teardown behavior both predate it; the deploy just triggered a pre-existing interaction, meaning every prior `CLIENT_VERSION` bump in this project's history likely caused the same disruption for any backgrounded tab, just never caught before. Fixed with a shared `reloadNowOrWhenVisible()` helper (`progress.js`, near `checkClientVersion`): reloads immediately if `!document.hidden` (unchanged behavior for the common case), otherwise registers a one-time `visibilitychange` listener and defers until the tab is actually foregrounded again — a `reloadPendingOnVisible` flag stops repeated polls while still hidden from stacking duplicate listeners. Applied to both reload sites (`checkClientVersion` and `scheduleMidnightReload`'s `fire`), since both share the identical mechanism and therefore the identical bug. While backgrounded, no reload means no beacon at all, so a genuinely-running session is never touched — the update simply lands the next time the student glances back at the tab (even briefly), rather than never arriving or silently killing an untouched session; for the rarer case where a tab genuinely never becomes visible again until the session ends naturally, that's a strictly better outcome than the alternative (a session that runs to completion undisturbed, vs. one silently killed mid-session by an uninvited reload). Verified directly via CDP against a real headless Chrome session (not reasoned about): overrode `document.hidden` and intercepted `/api/version` to force a deterministic mismatch, then tracked real `Page.frameNavigated` events (not a JS-level stub — `location.reload` turned out to be non-configurable in this Chrome build, so stubbing it directly threw and had to be abandoned in favor of watching for an actual navigation) — confirmed zero navigation while hidden, exactly one navigation firing the moment visibility was restored, and — as a regression check — exactly one immediate navigation when a mismatch is detected while already visible, matching the pre-existing behavior for the common case. **One unavoidable bootstrapping gap, called out rather than silently accepted**: the very deploy that ships this fix still causes the old behavior one more time for any tab that's already open at that moment, since those tabs are still running the pre-fix JS until they reload — every deploy after this one is safe, this one isn't fully.
- **No `[[headers]]` block existed in `netlify.toml` until 2026-08**, so every static asset (images, favicons, the vendor React bundle) inherited Netlify's default `Cache-Control: public, max-age=0, must-revalidate` — the same "always revalidate, never cache" policy meant for HTML — forcing every repeat visit to re-download them in full. This was the single largest driver of Web bandwidth credits (checked via the account's Usage panel, not a guess). Fixed with a 7-day cache for `*.webp`/`*.png`/`favicon.ico`/`vendor/*` only — deliberately excludes HTML and actively-edited JS/CSS (`progress.js`, `blogs/blog.css`/`blog.js`) so shipped fixes keep showing up immediately instead of being stuck behind a stale cache. **`netlify dev`'s local "simple static server" fallback doesn't apply custom `[[headers]]`** even after a restart (confirmed: `curl -I` against localhost:8080 kept showing `max-age=0` post-restart) — this isn't a sign the config is broken, it's a known local-dev-only limitation; the production CDN does honor it. Don't waste time trying to verify cache headers via localhost.
- **The shared nav's mobile-nav breakpoint (`max-width: 1024px`, one identical rule copy-pasted across all 7 main pages) was too low for real iPad landscape widths, cutting off Contact Us** ("in the landscape mode of ipad the contact us portion of the nav bar is cut off," 2026-09-14). iPad models don't share one landscape width — regular iPad/mini land at exactly 1024px (already correctly caught by the old breakpoint, switching to the hamburger menu), but iPad Air (10.9", 1180px) and iPad Pro 11" (1194px) both exceed 1024px, so they got the *desktop* nav (5 links + Sign In/Up + a separate Contact Us button) instead — and that desktop nav's actual content doesn't fit until ~1290px wide. Measured directly with a real viewport sweep (not guessed): at 1180-1194px the Contact Us button's right edge lands 85-100px past the visible viewport, i.e. almost entirely off-screen; the overflow doesn't disappear until 1290px. Fixed by raising the breakpoint to `1300px` (a small safety margin past the measured 1290px) on all 7 pages — `index.html`, `gate-da-courses.html`, `gate-da-test-series.html`, `gate-da-toppers.html`, `contact.html`, `gate-da-progress-tracker.html`, `gate-da-free-notes.html` — same "shared component, fix everywhere" discipline as the earlier nav-logo hover-jitter fix. **Each page also has its own unrelated `max-width: 1024px` rules** (page-specific content-stacking breakpoints like `gate-da-courses.html`'s `.incl-layout`/`.whyus-layout`, plus two pages' own JS `matchMedia('(max-width: 1024px)')` calls gating animation timing, unrelated to the nav) — confirmed by inspecting each occurrence individually before editing, and touched only the one exact nav rule per page, leaving every other `1024px` reference (CSS or JS) untouched. Verified with a real viewport-sweep + interaction test on two of the seven pages (`gate-da-progress-tracker.html`, `index.html`): both iPad Air and iPad Pro 11" now correctly show the hamburger menu with Contact Us fully reachable inside it (confirmed by actually opening the mobile menu and checking the item's bounding rect stays within the viewport, not just checking `display`), while a real laptop width (1440px) and iPad Pro 12.9" (1366px, already fine before) both still correctly show the full desktop nav with nothing clipped — a genuine before/after regression check, not just re-confirming the one reported case.
  - **Follow-up the same day, at direct request ("make sure this navbar issue is not repeated in the future")**: the 1300px breakpoint above was still just a hardcoded pixel guess with a thin ~10px margin past the measured 1290px cliff — the moment the nav gains one more link, a longer label, or a wider button, the true cliff silently moves and the same class of bug can reappear with no warning. The actual fix: `.nav-links`' `gap: 36px` and `.nav-inner`'s `gap: 28px` (both on all 7 pages) became fluid — `clamp(18px, 2.2vw, 36px)` and `clamp(14px, 1.6vw, 28px)` respectively — so the nav's own footprint shrinks smoothly as the viewport narrows instead of staying a fixed size that either fits or doesn't. Re-measured after the change with the same real viewport-sweep technique (this time forcing the desktop nav's `display` on regardless of the mobile breakpoint, via an injected `<style>` override, specifically to see how narrow the content itself can go): zero overflow all the way down to 850px, where before this change the true minimum was 1290px — over 400px of genuine slack now, room to absorb a future 6th nav link or two without anyone needing to touch this again. `el.offsetLeft`/`el.offsetWidth`-based hover-pill positioning (`nav-pill`) reads the actual live layout at render time, not an assumed gap value, so it's unaffected by the gap becoming fluid — confirmed directly, not just reasoned about, by cross-checking `linksRef`'s measurement logic before making the change. Verified visually too: a real screenshot at 1440px (genuine desktop width) shows spacing indistinguishable from before (`2.2vw` doesn't hit its own `36px` ceiling until ~1636px, so typical laptop/desktop widths keep close to the original look); an 1180px screenshot with the desktop nav forced on showed the *content itself* fitting cleanly with room to spare (a cosmetic logo/link overlap in that specific screenshot was confirmed to be purely a test-harness artifact — from forcibly overriding `display` while the mobile logo-centering rule was still active from the untouched 1300px breakpoint — not a real production state, since real visitors at 1180px still correctly get the hamburger nav regardless). The 1300px breakpoint itself was left as-is; it no longer needs to be exactly right, since actual fit now has thick padding underneath it rather than being the thing barely holding the layout together.
- **A shared scroll-reveal `show()` re-deriving "is this element already on-screen" reactively, inside the IntersectionObserver callback, is a tautology for any observer with no `rootMargin`** — found 2026-09-22 building the GATE 2028 card (see its own section above), but the bug lives in `gate-da-courses.html`'s general-purpose reveal `React.useEffect`, not anything specific to that card. `show()`'s "skip the entrance animation if this element was already visible" check used to call `getBoundingClientRect()` at the moment each observer's callback fired — which happens to still read correctly for the site's normal `.fade-in` observer, since its 200px early `rootMargin` means the callback usually fires just *before* the element is actually on-screen. But a margin-less observer's callback can only ever fire once the element already has real on-screen overlap (that's what `isIntersecting: true` means at a positive threshold with no margin) — so the "is it on-screen right now" recheck was always true for it, meaning the entrance animation silently skipped straight to its final state on every single scroll, never visibly playing. Fixed by computing "was this on-screen at page load" once per element, synchronously, into a `WeakSet` before any observer starts watching anything, instead of re-deriving it live inside `show()`. Worth checking first if any other `.fade-in` element on this page (or any of the other 6, if this pattern gets copied) is ever given its own observer without the shared 200px margin — that combination reliably produces "the animation just doesn't play, but nothing errors and nothing looks obviously broken in a static screenshot."
- **A `clamp(MIN, Nvw, MAX)` font-size silently stops being responsive below whatever viewport width first makes `Nvw` reach `MIN`** — found 2026-09-22 on the GATE 2028 card's "Coming Soon" chip (`clamp(22px, 5.5vw, 30px)`, `5.5vw` reaches its own 22px floor at ~400px viewport width), but the failure mode is generic to any `clamp()` used this way on this site. Below that width the value is pinned flat at `MIN` — it does **not** keep shrinking — while whatever box the text sits in usually keeps shrinking right along with the viewport, so a value tuned to "fits fine on a normal phone" can silently start overflowing/clipping on a narrower one with zero warning (confirmed: 253px of unwrapped text squeezed into a 224px box at 390px viewport, clipping mid-word). If a `clamp()`-sized element's container also shrinks with viewport width, verify the low end explicitly against real narrow widths (down to ~320px) rather than trusting the `vw` term to keep scaling down — it stops working exactly where phones get most likely to need it.

## Future considerations (not started, no code exists for this yet)

**Cutting the live pages over from CSV to `site_pricing`/`site_notes`/`site_lectures`, floated 2026-09-22, explicitly deferred — `gate-da-courses.html`'s pricing is now DONE (2026-09-23, see the full writeup in its own section above); `site_notes`/`site_lectures` and `gate-da-test-series.html`/`index.html`/`gate-da-free-notes.html` are still deferred.** The `gate-da-courses.html` cutover proved out the exact pattern the rest can reuse: a public, unauthenticated read endpoint (`site-pricing-public.js`) returning every public-safe column, the live page merging it over its existing CSV/hardcoded data (Supabase wins per-field where it has a value, CSV/hardcoded stays the fallback), so a migration or fetch failure can only ever leave things exactly as they were, never break the page. `gate-da-test-series.html` also reads `PRICING_CSV_URL` (its own `planPricing()`, same CSV as `gate-da-courses.html`), so extending this same cutover there — reading `site-pricing-public.js` and merging over the CSV — is the most natural next step, same pattern, different page, not yet done. `site_notes`/`site_lectures` → `gate-da-free-notes.html` haven't been started either.

**Cost/usage analysis, 2026-09-06** — triggered by a Netlify usage screenshot (Functions compute the dominant line item) and a Supabase usage screenshot (Egress the dominant line item there, 1.442/5GB = 29%, everything else near 0% since this project doesn't use Supabase Auth/Storage/Realtime/Edge Functions at all today). Led to modeling what happens at a hypothetical 300 active students (up from the real ~52 active / 302 total at the time), which reversed the initial instinct on what to fix:

- **Splitting some Netlify Functions to Supabase Edge Functions was floated first, then rejected as the wrong fix.** The mechanism would have worked (a Netlify `[[redirects]]` rule can proxy a path straight to an external URL with zero frontend changes), and `pomodoro-leaderboard.js` (polled every 30s during Focus Mode, no admin auth, the single highest-frequency call in the app) was the identified candidate. **But it doesn't reduce Supabase Egress — likely the opposite.** Today, a request flows browser → Netlify Function → Supabase (query) → Netlify Function → browser; Supabase's Egress meter only counts the middle leg. Moving the function onto Supabase's own Edge Functions means the *entire* response, including the final leg back to the browser, now leaves Supabase's network directly — adding a new chunk of traffic to the meter that's already the real constraint. **Verdict: don't do this.** It solves a problem (Netlify Functions compute) that degrades gracefully at scale, while leaving untouched, or worsening, the one that doesn't (Egress).
- **The real finding: at 300 active students, Egress — not Netlify compute — exhausts the entire monthly free-tier allowance in 1-3 days, not the month.** Modeled by measuring real per-call byte sizes for `pomodoro-leaderboard.js` against live production data (~43.5 KB/call today) and scaling by session-length assumptions; the model's 1-hour/day prediction at the real current 52-active-student count landed within a few days of the real observed 1.442 GB Egress figure, which is a solid confidence check on the methodology.
- **Measured (not guessed) breakdown of that 43.5 KB**: streak-related queries (`scheduledForStreak` + `completedForStreak`) are 33,081 bytes, last-week-rank data (`lastWeekStats`, unbounded — fetches *every* active student's prior week, no `LIMIT`) is 3,568 bytes. Together, **84.2% of every single leaderboard poll's payload** is spent on these two pieces; the genuinely-must-stay-live parts (this week's/today's stats, live status, display names) are only 15.8%, and are already bounded by `LIMIT 10`/`LIMIT 20` clauses so they don't scale with total student count anyway.
- **Database Size is NOT a real concern, structurally, not just because the numbers are currently small.** `pomo_daily_sessions` grows by at most one row per active student per day (an upsert, not a per-completion insert), so it scales with (active students × elapsed days), not with poll frequency or session length the way Egress does. Even at 300 active students for a full year, that's ~18 MB against the 500 MB free-tier cap. Every other table grows even slower (bounded by course content, or weekly instead of daily). Don't re-litigate this one without new information — the growth curve is fundamentally different from Egress's.
- **A "batch-credit everyone every 25 minutes instead of writing on each completion" idea was floated for the write side and rejected** — it targets the wrong path entirely (writes were never the bottleneck; reads via the leaderboard poll are), and is structurally broken regardless: students' Pomodoro sessions aren't synchronized to any shared clock (work-phase duration is customizable per student, 1-180 min), so a fixed global poll would either credit sessions late by up to the poll interval (breaking the immediate-feedback timer/chime) or need to run so often it wouldn't save anything. The current event-driven, write-on-actual-completion model is already correct and cheap; don't revisit this direction.

**The actual plan, in priority order (none of this started as of 2026-09-06):**
1. ~~Cache last week's rank.~~ **Done, 2026-09-06** — see `weekly-rank-snapshot.js` in the Progress tracker section above for the full writeup, including a real tie-handling bug (`RANK()` vs `ROW_NUMBER()`) caught before it shipped.
2. ~~Cache streak.~~ **Done, 2026-09-06** — `students.current_streak`, kept correct by two write-side triggers plus all three read sites now switched over. **Write side**: `daily-streak-snapshot.js` (runs `00:15` IST daily, same self-healing/idempotent daily-check reasoning as `weekly-rank-snapshot.js`, catches a streak breaking from a day passing with no action) + `complete-task.js`'s own recompute-and-write right after its existing upsert (catches the opposite case — today's completion showing up immediately, not tomorrow; awaited before responding, not fired-and-forgotten, since Netlify Functions don't reliably keep running background work after a response is sent; wrapped in its own try/catch so a streak-update failure can never fail the already-saved task completion). Building this surfaced a real bug caught before shipping (a partial-column `.upsert()` fails outright against a NOT NULL column like `display_name` — Postgres validates the candidate insert row before it even checks for a conflict — fixed with a real `UPDATE` via a Postgres function instead, `update_student_streaks`, confirmed safe on a disposable test student first) **and a second, bigger one already live in production**: `team-students.js`'s unbounded `task_progress`/`pomo_daily_sessions` queries were silently truncated by PostgREST's 1,000-row response cap (real counts: 2,595 and 1,112), so `/team`'s Students view had been computing streak, all-time hours, last-active date, and consistency from an incomplete slice of real data the whole time, with no error. Both fixed via a new shared `fetchAllRows()` helper (`lib/supabase.js`, pages with `.range()`). Verified extensively on a disposable test student against real scheduled dates: multi-day completion (streak grew 0→2→3, matching `computeStreak`'s actual grace-doesn't-extend-past-a-gap semantics, not a naive expectation), unticking a middle day to create a gap, double-submit idempotency, and the response shape (`{ok:true}`, unchanged) — every cached value cross-checked against an independent fresh computation, all matched. **Read side**: `pomodoro-leaderboard.js`, `streak.js`, and `team-students.js` all read `students.current_streak` directly now instead of recomputing from `schedule_tasks`+`task_progress` per request — `pomodoro-leaderboard.js` dropped its own `scheduledForStreak`/`completedForStreak` queries entirely (the 33 KB, 76% of that endpoint's payload, the earlier egress breakdown above measured for exactly this), `team-students.js` dropped its `schedule_tasks` query outright since streak was its only consumer, `streak.js` collapsed to a single indexed `students` lookup. No `CLIENT_VERSION` bump needed — server-only computation change, response shape unchanged, confirmed by diffing old-vs-new output field-for-field against real production data before cutover (zero diffs across all 308 students on `team-students.js`, all 20 leaderboard rows + today's leaders on `pomodoro-leaderboard.js`, and several real students plus a nonexistent-email edge case on `streak.js`) — same discipline as the last-week-rank switch. `streak.js`'s cache-freshness guarantee for the task-toggle path specifically: `progress.js`'s `refreshStreak()` only fires after its `/api/complete-task` call resolves, and `complete-task.js` awaits its own `current_streak` write before responding, so the ordering is guaranteed, not incidental.
3. ~~Widen `LEADERBOARD_POLL_MS` from 30s to 60s.~~ **Done, 2026-09-06** — one-line change (`progress.js`), halves both Netlify compute and this endpoint's Egress immediately for every viewer, no scaling assumptions needed. Real (if modest) UX trade-off: the leaderboard/live-dot refreshes half as often; per-second countdown timers are unaffected since those already tick client-side from already-fetched data, independent of this poll. `CLIENT_VERSION` bumped (`2026-09-04-4` → `2026-09-06-1`) since this is a real `progress.js` runtime-behavior change — a tab already open mid-Pomodoro-session picks up the new interval via an automatic reload within `VERSION_CHECK_MS` (2 min), safely: `checkClientVersion()`'s reload already correctly restores Focus Mode and the running timer (see `wasHardReload`/`restorePomoActiveState` in the Progress tracker section above), and skips reloading entirely while the settings panel is open so an unsaved duration edit isn't yanked away — it just catches the mismatch on the next poll instead. No other client-facing change shipped alongside it, so this reload is the only thing a live user experiences from this deploy.
4. **Deferred, correctly scoped as a bigger project, not started**: replace the poll with genuine push-based updates (Supabase Realtime) — the real structural fix, since data moved would become proportional to what actually changes instead of to how often anyone happens to be polling. Concretely harder than 1-3, and a different category of work: it needs new RLS policies to let the browser subscribe directly (a real security change, this project currently keeps 100% of Supabase access behind `service_role`-gated functions, with the browser never holding direct DB credentials at all), a new client-side Supabase dependency, a genuine logic port (Realtime streams raw row changes, not the joined/computed/formatted results the server currently produces — rank sorting, the live-status time check, streak-ball capping would all need reimplementing in the browser), and reconnection/fallback handling for dropped WebSocket connections. Correctly deferred until actually approaching a scale where fixes 1-3 stop being enough — building it ahead of that need means carrying real security risk and maintenance surface for a problem that doesn't exist yet.

**Multi-batch schedule support, floated 2026-09-08, explicitly not near-term** — the question was "shouldn't we store batch-wise schedules in Supabase so it's easier to change without redeploying whole code," which conflates two different things worth keeping separate for next time this comes up: schedule *content* is already fully in Supabase (`schedule_tasks`, loaded via `npm run load-schedule`) and has never needed a deploy to change; what's actually missing is any `batch` dimension at all — no `batch` column on `schedule_tasks` or `students`, so every student sees the identical schedule regardless of which physical batch they're in, and the only thing genuinely hardcoded in `progress.js` is the "TAAI BATCH C" page-title string (cosmetic, not schedule data). Real multi-batch support would need: a `batch` column on both `schedule_tasks` and `students`, a way for a student to declare their batch at registration, every query that reads `schedule_tasks`/`task_progress` filtered by it, and an explicit decision on whether the leaderboard stays one global list or splits per batch (currently undecided — a real product question, not just an engineering one). Confirmed with the user this is not near-term; noted here rather than started.
